Well… yes, I suppose that’s fair. I came in because my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten a bit too tight, though to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly.

I think it’s more that things feel wrong if they aren’t measured properly… though, of course, I don’t like feeling that my clothes are tight either. It all gets mixed together.

I came in because my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly. Breakfast is yogurt because it’s straightforward, lunch sometimes doesn’t happen if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured carefully so it all sort of balances.

It reminds me of when I used to work on scheduling models—if one number was off, the whole thing felt untidy. So I suppose it’s not only appearance. It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.

It’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.

So yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.

I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.

At home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? But yes, the food planning does connect to that, to how my shape feels in clothes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your shape or weight influences how you feel about yourself overall, or does it play a large role in how you judge yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I mean, I’m not trying to look like I’m thirty again or anything ridiculous. I know I’m an older woman.\n\nBut if my waist and hips look contained in my clothes, then I feel like I’m handling myself properly. Like things are in order. It’s funny, I used to be very particular about spreadsheets being lined up just so—columns, margins, all of that—and I think I do that with myself too. If my shape feels neat, I feel better about myself. If it doesn’t, I feel… not quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself sick or used laxatives or diet pills to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve always been careful with routines—lists, measurements, that sort of thing. Even when I was working, I’d get very absorbed in making sure everything balanced properly, sometimes more than other people thought was necessary.\n\nBut making myself sick, or pills… I don’t really think of that as part of what I do. Laxatives, diet pills—no, that’s not something I’ve used for that. It’s more the planning and keeping things measured.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever skipped meals, fasted, or followed very strict dietary rules to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose that’s fair. I came in because my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten a bit too tight, though to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly.\n\nBreakfast is usually just yogurt, because that’s simple and accounted for. Lunch I’ll skip if I’m busy—when I was working with models and schedules, I could lose hours that way too, just keeping things aligned. And then dinner is measured out carefully, so the whole day feels balanced. I don’t think of it as fasting exactly, but yes, I do have rules about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you follow these dietary rules mainly because you feel you must control your body shape, or do you feel a sense of discomfort or anxiety if things aren’t measured just right, regardless of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that things feel wrong if they aren’t measured properly… though, of course, I don’t like feeling that my clothes are tight either. It all gets mixed together.\n\nI came in because my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly. Breakfast is yogurt because it’s straightforward, lunch sometimes doesn’t happen if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured carefully so it all sort of balances.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to work on scheduling models—if one number was off, the whole thing felt untidy. So I suppose it’s not only appearance. It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, everyone has odd thoughts pop in now and then, don’t they? Like remembering something embarrassing you said years ago while you’re trying to file paperwork, that sort of thing.

When I worked on routing problems, I’d sometimes wake up thinking about a constraint I’d forgotten, but that was more my brain continuing the problem, not something frightening. It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. At least nothing that stands out.

It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or find upsetting, even if you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. I mean, I might choose the more familiar errand route because the parking is easier, but that’s not… fear, exactly. It’s just efficient.

It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or find upsetting, even if you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, everyone has odd thoughts pop in now and then, don’t they? Like remembering something embarrassing you said years ago while you’re trying to file paperwork, that sort of thing.\n\nWhen I worked on routing problems, I’d sometimes wake up thinking about a constraint I’d forgotten, but that was more my brain continuing the problem, not something frightening. It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. At least nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any thoughts or mental images that come into your mind that feel foreign or upsetting to you, like they don’t really belong to who you are, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. “Foreign” is an interesting word… it makes me think of when I used to proofread a model and there’d be one variable that just didn’t belong, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI can get stuck thinking about things, certainly, but it feels like my own mind being overly busy, not images or thoughts that feel alien to me or upsetting in that way. I can’t think of anything like that, at least not offhand.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or distressed when you can't control or predict how something will turn out, even if it's not related to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. Of course, if a shipment schedule changed at the last minute, everyone would get irritated, not just me.\n\nAt home… if something doesn’t go according to plan, I might feel thrown off for a bit. But I can’t think of anything outside the usual frustration, really. Not something where I’d say I’m distressed because I can’t control it. I may be overthinking the wording.\n\nDoctor: When things don’t go according to plan, do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because you’re worried they’ll feel too unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll check the door once before bed, but that seems normal—especially because my neighbor had a package taken once, and after that everyone on the block got a bit fussy about locks for a while.

Counting… not really. I worked with numbers for years, so I do notice patterns, I suppose, like if tiles are uneven or if a spreadsheet column is misaligned, but I don’t feel like I have to fix it to make the world feel right. I just like things orderly.\n\nUsually I’ll have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly, work used to be like that too, I was an operations research analyst, so planning and schedules sort of stick with you. Then dinner is just measured out carefully so the day feels under control. My doctor thinks that’s worth talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you plan your meals or control your food intake because you're concerned about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, partly. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look young or anything like that—I’m 63, I know what age I am. But I do notice whether my waist and hips look, you know, contained in my clothes. If things feel neat there, I feel like I’m managing myself properly.  \n\nIt’s a bit like keeping a desk organized, I suppose—though my husband used to say my idea of “organized” was too much. But yes, the food planning does connect to that, to how my shape feels in clothes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your shape or weight influences how you feel about yourself overall, or does it play a large role in how you judge yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I mean, I’m not trying to look like I’m thirty again or anything ridiculous. I know I’m an older woman.\n\nBut if my waist and hips look contained in my clothes, then I feel like I’m handling myself properly. Like things are in order. It’s funny, I used to be very particular about spreadsheets being lined up just so—columns, margins, all of that—and I think I do that with myself too. If my shape feels neat, I feel better about myself. If it doesn’t, I feel… not quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself sick or used laxatives or diet pills to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve always been careful with routines—lists, measurements, that sort of thing. Even when I was working, I’d get very absorbed in making sure everything balanced properly, sometimes more than other people thought was necessary.\n\nBut making myself sick, or pills… I don’t really think of that as part of what I do. Laxatives, diet pills—no, that’s not something I’ve used for that. It’s more the planning and keeping things measured.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever skipped meals, fasted, or followed very strict dietary rules to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose that’s fair. I came in because my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten a bit too tight, though to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly.\n\nBreakfast is usually just yogurt, because that’s simple and accounted for. Lunch I’ll skip if I’m busy—when I was working with models and schedules, I could lose hours that way too, just keeping things aligned. And then dinner is measured out carefully, so the whole day feels balanced. I don’t think of it as fasting exactly, but yes, I do have rules about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you follow these dietary rules mainly because you feel you must control your body shape, or do you feel a sense of discomfort or anxiety if things aren’t measured just right, regardless of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that things feel wrong if they aren’t measured properly… though, of course, I don’t like feeling that my clothes are tight either. It all gets mixed together.\n\nI came in because my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly. Breakfast is yogurt because it’s straightforward, lunch sometimes doesn’t happen if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured carefully so it all sort of balances.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to work on scheduling models—if one number was off, the whole thing felt untidy. So I suppose it’s not only appearance. It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or find upsetting, even if you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, everyone has odd thoughts pop in now and then, don’t they? Like remembering something embarrassing you said years ago while you’re trying to file paperwork, that sort of thing.\n\nWhen I worked on routing problems, I’d sometimes wake up thinking about a constraint I’d forgotten, but that was more my brain continuing the problem, not something frightening. It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. At least nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any thoughts or mental images that come into your mind that feel foreign or upsetting to you, like they don’t really belong to who you are, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. “Foreign” is an interesting word… it makes me think of when I used to proofread a model and there’d be one variable that just didn’t belong, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI can get stuck thinking about things, certainly, but it feels like my own mind being overly busy, not images or thoughts that feel alien to me or upsetting in that way. I can’t think of anything like that, at least not offhand.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or distressed when you can't control or predict how something will turn out, even if it's not related to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. In my old work, uncertainty was sort of the whole point—you’d make models because things didn’t behave neatly. Of course, if a shipment schedule changed at the last minute, everyone would get irritated, not just me.\n\nAt home… if something doesn’t go according to plan, I might feel thrown off for a bit. But I can’t think of anything outside the usual frustration, really. Not something where I’d say I’m distressed because I can’t control it. I may be overthinking the wording.\n\nDoctor: When things don’t go according to plan, do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because you’re worried they’ll feel too unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. I mean, I might choose the more familiar errand route because the parking is easier, but that’s not… fear, exactly. It’s just efficient.\n\nIn operations work you’re always trying to reduce unnecessary variability—though I suppose that sounds like control when I say it that way. But in daily life, if something might be unpredictable, I don’t think I’m staying away from it because of that. I can get annoyed if plans change, but avoiding whole situations? No, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that bother you so much that you feel you have to push them out of your mind or do something to neutralize them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a thought I’d rather not dwell on, the way anyone can—like remembering I forgot to return a library book years ago, which is silly, because I don’t even use that branch anymore—but I don’t feel like I have to do some special thing to cancel it out.\n\n“Neutralize” makes me think of chemistry class, actually. Or balancing an equation. But no, I don’t think I have thoughts or images that bother me in that particular way. If something nags at me, I might distract myself or get busy with something else, but not because I feel compelled to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing certain things over and over again—like checking, counting, or arranging—in a specific way that feels necessary to reduce discomfort or make things feel 'right'?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll check the door once before bed, but that seems normal—especially because my neighbor had a package taken once, and after that everyone on the block got a bit fussy about locks for a while.\n\nCounting… not really. I worked with numbers for years, so I do notice patterns, I suppose, like if tiles are uneven or if a spreadsheet column is misaligned, but I don’t feel like I have to fix it to make the world feel right. It’s more that sloppy formatting is annoying. My old supervisor used to leave these reports with decimal places all over the place—two here, four there—and it made me want to tidy them up, but that was work, not some… compulsion.\n\nSo no, I can’t think of anything I repeat in a specific way to reduce discomfort. Not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "F43.22 (Adjustment disorder with anxiety) is ruled out as the patient's symptoms are more chronic and not primarily linked to a specific stressor. The focus remains on eating behaviors and obsessive-compulsive traits, supporting the retention of F50.00 (Anorexia nervosa) and F42.9 (Obsessive-compulsive disorder, unspecified).",
  "is_final": false
}

It’s just efficient.\n\nIn operations work you’re always trying to reduce unnecessary variability—though I suppose that sounds like control when I say it that way. But in daily life, if something might be unpredictable, I don’t think I’m staying away from it because of that. I can get annoyed if plans change, but avoiding whole situations? No, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that bother you so much that you feel you have to push them out of your mind or do something to neutralize them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a thought I’d rather not dwell on, the way anyone can—like remembering I forgot to return a library book years ago, which is silly, because I don’t even use that branch anymore—but I don’t feel like I have to do some special thing to cancel it out.\n\n“Neutralize” makes me think of chemistry class, actually. Or balancing an equation. But no, I don’t think I have thoughts or images that bother me in that particular way. If something nags at me, I might distract myself or get busy with something else, but not because I feel compelled to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing certain things over and over again—like checking, counting, or arranging—in a specific way that feels necessary to reduce discomfort or make things feel 'right'?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll check the door once before bed, but that seems normal—especially because my neighbor had a package taken once, and after that everyone on the block got a bit fussy about locks for a while.\n\nCounting… not really. I worked with numbers for years, so I do notice patterns, I suppose, like if tiles are uneven or if a spreadsheet column is misaligned, but I don’t feel like I have to fix it to make the world feel right. It’s more that sloppy formatting is annoying. My old supervisor used to leave these reports with decimal places all over the place—two here, four there—and it made me want to tidy them up, but that was work, not some… compulsion.\n\nSo no, I can’t think of anything I repeat in a specific way to reduce discomfort. Not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that feel so disturbing or unwanted that you try hard to get rid of them, even if they don’t relate to food or your shape?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / operations_research_analyst / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get an unpleasant thought, like anyone—something embarrassing I said years ago will pop up while I’m brushing my teeth, and then I’m annoyed because why would my brain choose that moment, you know?

But disturbing in the sense that I’m trying hard to force it out, no. Usually if something unwanted comes up, I just shift to whatever I’m doing next. I make tea, or check the mail, or—well, yesterday I spent ten minutes looking for the good scissors and then forgot what I was thinking about entirely. So… no, not really in the way you’re describing.

Then dinner is just measured out carefully so the day feels under control. My doctor thinks that’s worth talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you plan your meals or control your food intake because you're concerned about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, partly. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look young or anything like that—I’m 63, I know what age I am. But I do notice whether my waist and hips look, you know, contained in my clothes. If things feel neat there, I feel like I’m managing myself properly.  \n\nIt’s a bit like keeping a desk organized, I suppose—though my husband used to say my idea of “organized” was too much. But yes, the food planning does connect to that, to how my shape feels in clothes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your shape or weight influences how you feel about yourself overall, or does it play a large role in how you judge yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I mean, I’m not trying to look like I’m thirty again or anything ridiculous. I know I’m an older woman.\n\nBut if my waist and hips look contained in my clothes, then I feel like I’m handling myself properly. Like things are in order. It’s funny, I used to be very particular about spreadsheets being lined up just so—columns, margins, all of that—and I think I do that with myself too. If my shape feels neat, I feel better about myself. If it doesn’t, I feel… not quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself sick or used laxatives or diet pills to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve always been careful with routines—lists, measurements, that sort of thing. Laxatives, diet pills—no, that’s not something I’ve used for that. It’s more the planning and keeping things measured.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever skipped meals, fasted, or followed very strict dietary rules to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose that’s fair. I came in because my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten a bit too tight, though to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly.\n\nBreakfast is usually just yogurt, because that’s simple and accounted for. Lunch I’ll skip if I’m busy—when I was working with models and schedules, I could lose hours that way too, just keeping things aligned. And then dinner is measured out carefully, so the whole day feels balanced. I don’t think of it as fasting exactly, but yes, I do have rules about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you follow these dietary rules mainly because you feel you must control your body shape, or do you feel a sense of discomfort or anxiety if things aren’t measured just right, regardless of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that things feel wrong if they aren’t measured properly… though, of course, I don’t like feeling that my clothes are tight either. It all gets mixed together.\n\nI came in because my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly. Breakfast is yogurt because it’s straightforward, lunch sometimes doesn’t happen if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured carefully so it all sort of balances.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to work on scheduling models—if one number was off, the whole thing felt untidy. So I suppose it’s not only appearance. It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or find upsetting, even if you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, everyone has odd thoughts pop in now and then, don’t they? Like remembering something embarrassing you said years ago while you’re trying to file paperwork, that sort of thing.\n\nWhen I worked on routing problems, I’d sometimes wake up thinking about a constraint I’d forgotten, but that was more my brain continuing the problem, not something frightening. It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. I can’t think of anything like that, at least not offhand.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or distressed when you can't control or predict how something will turn out, even if it's not related to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. I may be overthinking the wording.\n\nDoctor: When things don’t go according to plan, do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because you’re worried they’ll feel too unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose it does, though not in a way that looks dramatic from the outside. I still get my analysis work done, and I run my errands—bank, post office, groceries, all that. I’ve always been pretty reliable about those things.

But I’ve mostly stopped going out for meals with my friends after book club. We used to do that, and it was nice, actually—there’s this little place near the library with terrible parking but good lighting, if that makes sense. But eating out throws off the plan I rely on, and then I’m sitting there trying to look normal while my mind is busy with all of that. So I usually just say I’m tired or I have something to do at home.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor asked me to come in, really. She seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten… too tight, I guess. I don’t quite see it that way. I just like things orderly.\n\nUsually I’ll have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly, work used to be like that too, I was an operations research analyst, so planning and schedules sort of stick with you. Then dinner is just measured out carefully so the day feels under control. My doctor thinks that’s worth talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you plan your meals or control your food intake because you're concerned about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, partly. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look young or anything like that—I’m 63, I know what age I am. But I do notice whether my waist and hips look, you know, contained in my clothes. If things feel neat there, I feel like I’m managing myself properly.  \n\nIt’s a bit like keeping a desk organized, I suppose—though my husband used to say my idea of “organized” was too much. But yes, the food planning does connect to that, to how my shape feels in clothes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your shape or weight influences how you feel about yourself overall, or does it play a large role in how you judge yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I mean, I’m not trying to look like I’m thirty again or anything ridiculous. I know I’m an older woman.\n\nBut if my waist and hips look contained in my clothes, then I feel like I’m handling myself properly. Like things are in order. It’s funny, I used to be very particular about spreadsheets being lined up just so—columns, margins, all of that—and I think I do that with myself too. If my shape feels neat, I feel better about myself. If it doesn’t, I feel… not quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself sick or used laxatives or diet pills to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve always been careful with routines—lists, measurements, that sort of thing. Even when I was working, I’d get very absorbed in making sure everything balanced properly, sometimes more than other people thought was necessary.\n\nBut making myself sick, or pills… I don’t really think of that as part of what I do. Laxatives, diet pills—no, that’s not something I’ve used for that. It’s more the planning and keeping things measured.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever skipped meals, fasted, or followed very strict dietary rules to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose that’s fair. I came in because my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten a bit too tight, though to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly.\n\nBreakfast is usually just yogurt, because that’s simple and accounted for. Lunch I’ll skip if I’m busy—when I was working with models and schedules, I could lose hours that way too, just keeping things aligned. And then dinner is measured out carefully, so the whole day feels balanced. I don’t think of it as fasting exactly, but yes, I do have rules about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you follow these dietary rules mainly because you feel you must control your body shape, or do you feel a sense of discomfort or anxiety if things aren’t measured just right, regardless of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that things feel wrong if they aren’t measured properly… though, of course, I don’t like feeling that my clothes are tight either. It all gets mixed together.\n\nI came in because my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly. Breakfast is yogurt because it’s straightforward, lunch sometimes doesn’t happen if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured carefully so it all sort of balances.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to work on scheduling models—if one number was off, the whole thing felt untidy. So I suppose it’s not only appearance. It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or find upsetting, even if you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, everyone has odd thoughts pop in now and then, don’t they? Like remembering something embarrassing you said years ago while you’re trying to file paperwork, that sort of thing.\n\nWhen I worked on routing problems, I’d sometimes wake up thinking about a constraint I’d forgotten, but that was more my brain continuing the problem, not something frightening. It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. At least nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any thoughts or mental images that come into your mind that feel foreign or upsetting to you, like they don’t really belong to who you are, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. “Foreign” is an interesting word… it makes me think of when I used to proofread a model and there’d be one variable that just didn’t belong, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI can get stuck thinking about things, certainly, but it feels like my own mind being overly busy, not images or thoughts that feel alien to me or upsetting in that way. I can’t think of anything like that, at least not offhand.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or distressed when you can't control or predict how something will turn out, even if it's not related to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. In my old work, uncertainty was sort of the whole point—you’d make models because things didn’t behave neatly. Of course, if a shipment schedule changed at the last minute, everyone would get irritated, not just me.\n\nAt home… if something doesn’t go according to plan, I might feel thrown off for a bit. But I can’t think of anything outside the usual frustration, really. Not something where I’d say I’m distressed because I can’t control it. I may be overthinking the wording.\n\nDoctor: When things don’t go according to plan, do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because you’re worried they’ll feel too unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. I mean, I might choose the more familiar errand route because the parking is easier, but that’s not… fear, exactly. It’s just efficient.\n\nIn operations work you’re always trying to reduce unnecessary variability—though I suppose that sounds like control when I say it that way. But in daily life, if something might be unpredictable, I don’t think I’m staying away from it because of that. I can get annoyed if plans change, but avoiding whole situations? No, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that bother you so much that you feel you have to push them out of your mind or do something to neutralize them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a thought I’d rather not dwell on, the way anyone can—like remembering I forgot to return a library book years ago, which is silly, because I don’t even use that branch anymore—but I don’t feel like I have to do some special thing to cancel it out.\n\n“Neutralize” makes me think of chemistry class, actually. I don’t think of it as fasting exactly, but yes, I do have rules about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you follow these dietary rules mainly because you feel you must control your body shape, or do you feel a sense of discomfort or anxiety if things aren’t measured just right, regardless of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that things feel wrong if they aren’t measured properly… though, of course, I don’t like feeling that my clothes are tight either. It all gets mixed together.\n\nI came in because my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels like keeping the day orderly. Breakfast is yogurt because it’s straightforward, lunch sometimes doesn’t happen if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured carefully so it all sort of balances.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to work on scheduling models—if one number was off, the whole thing felt untidy. So I suppose it’s not only appearance. It’s that discomfort when the plan isn’t exact.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your shape, eating habits, and the need for things to be measured just right cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I still manage most things, I think. I keep up with my analysis work and the usual errands—shopping, post office, all of that. I’ve always been quite organized with those things.\n\nIt’s more social things that have gotten… narrower, I suppose. After book club, people often go for a meal, and I used to join them. Lately I usually don’t, because it throws off the plan I’ve set for the day. And then everyone wants to split dishes or choose spontaneously, which sounds pleasant in theory, but it makes the whole evening feel unsettled for me.\n\nSo yes, it interferes some. Not everywhere, but enough that I’ve stopped doing certain things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having repeated, unwanted thoughts that bother you, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over again in a specific way to feel right or reduce discomfort—things that might not necessarily relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do like things done in a certain order, but I’ve always been that way. In operations research you get used to sequences and checking things—if a schedule is off by even a little, it can ripple through everything.\n\nAt home, I suppose I’ll straighten papers or make sure bills are stacked properly, but I don’t know that I’d call it unwanted thoughts. It’s more… habit? I did have a neighbor once who would check her stove over and over before leaving, and I remember thinking that seemed exhausting. Mine doesn’t feel quite like that.\n\nSo, outside of the food measuring and planning, I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or find upsetting, even if you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, everyone has odd thoughts pop in now and then, don’t they? Like remembering something embarrassing you said years ago while you’re trying to file paperwork, that sort of thing.\n\nWhen I worked on routing problems, I’d sometimes wake up thinking about a constraint I’d forgotten, but that was more my brain continuing the problem, not something frightening. It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. At least nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any thoughts or mental images that come into your mind that feel foreign or upsetting to you, like they don’t really belong to who you are, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. “Foreign” is an interesting word… it makes me think of when I used to proofread a model and there’d be one variable that just didn’t belong, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI can get stuck thinking about things, certainly, but it feels like my own mind being overly busy, not images or thoughts that feel alien to me or upsetting in that way. I can’t think of anything like that, at least not offhand.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or distressed when you can't control or predict how something will turn out, even if it's not related to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. In my old work, uncertainty was sort of the whole point—you’d make models because things didn’t behave neatly. Of course, if a shipment schedule changed at the last minute, everyone would get irritated, not just me.\n\nAt home… if something doesn’t go according to plan, I might feel thrown off for a bit. But I can’t think of anything outside the usual frustration, really. Not something where I’d say I’m distressed because I can’t control it. I may be overthinking the wording.\n\nDoctor: When things don’t go according to plan, do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because you’re worried they’ll feel too unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. I mean, I might choose the more familiar errand route because the parking is easier, but that’s not… fear, exactly. It’s just efficient.\n\nIn operations work you’re always trying to reduce unnecessary variability—though I suppose that sounds like control when I say it that way. But in daily life, if something might be unpredictable, I don’t think I’m staying away from it because of that. I can get annoyed if plans change, but avoiding whole situations? No, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that bother you so much that you feel you have to push them out of your mind or do something to neutralize them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a thought I’d rather not dwell on, the way anyone can—like remembering I forgot to return a library book years ago, which is silly, because I don’t even use that branch anymore—but I don’t feel like I have to do some special thing to cancel it out.\n\n“Neutralize” makes me think of chemistry class, actually. Or balancing an equation. But no, I don’t think I have thoughts or images that bother me in that particular way. If something nags at me, I might distract myself or get busy with something else, but not because I feel compelled to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing certain things over and over again—like checking, counting, or arranging—in a specific way that feels necessary to reduce discomfort or make things feel 'right'?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll check the door once before bed, but that seems normal—especially because my neighbor had a package taken once, and after that everyone on the block got a bit fussy about locks for a while.\n\nCounting… not really. I worked with numbers for years, so I do notice patterns, I suppose, like if tiles are uneven or if a spreadsheet column is misaligned, but I don’t feel like I have to fix it to make the world feel right. It’s more that sloppy formatting is annoying. My old supervisor used to leave these reports with decimal places all over the place—two here, four there—and it made me want to tidy them up, but that was work, not some… compulsion.\n\nSo no, I can’t think of anything I repeat in a specific way to reduce discomfort. Not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that feel so disturbing or unwanted that you try hard to get rid of them, even if they don’t relate to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get an unpleasant thought, like anyone—something embarrassing I said years ago will pop up while I’m brushing my teeth, and then I’m annoyed because why would my brain choose that moment, you know?\n\nBut disturbing in the sense that I’m trying hard to force it out, no. Usually if something unwanted comes up, I just shift to whatever I’m doing next. I make tea, or check the mail, or—well, yesterday I spent ten minutes looking for the good scissors and then forgot what I was thinking about entirely. So… no, not really in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about keeping your meals and shape under control cause you a lot of inner struggle, even if others might not see it, or make it hard for you to do things you care about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, though not in a way that looks dramatic from the outside. I still get my analysis work done, and I run my errands—bank, post office, groceries, all that. I’ve always been pretty reliable about those things.\n\nBut I’ve mostly stopped going out for meals with my friends after book club. We used to do that, and it was nice, actually—there’s this little place near the library with terrible parking but good lighting, if that makes sense. But eating out throws off the plan I rely on, and then I’m sitting there trying to look normal while my mind is busy with all of that. So I usually just say I’m tired or I have something to do at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d call them mental images, no. I think about food and keeping things controlled, of course, but it’s more… planning, or checking in with myself, not some awful picture barging in that I have to shove away.

It’s hard for me to separate what’s “unwanted” from what’s just mental clutter.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed upsetting thoughts that I’m trying to suppress. At least nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any thoughts or mental images that come into your mind that feel foreign or upsetting to you, like they don’t really belong to who you are, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. “Foreign” is an interesting word… it makes me think of when I used to proofread a model and there’d be one variable that just didn’t belong, but that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI can get stuck thinking about things, certainly, but it feels like my own mind being overly busy, not images or thoughts that feel alien to me or upsetting in that way. I can’t think of anything like that, at least not offhand.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or distressed when you can't control or predict how something will turn out, even if it's not related to food or your shape?\n\nPatient: I suppose I like knowing what’s coming, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious or distressed. In my old work, uncertainty was sort of the whole point—you’d make models because things didn’t behave neatly. Of course, if a shipment schedule changed at the last minute, everyone would get irritated, not just me.\n\nAt home… if something doesn’t go according to plan, I might feel thrown off for a bit. But I can’t think of anything outside the usual frustration, really. Not something where I’d say I’m distressed because I can’t control it. I may be overthinking the wording.\n\nDoctor: When things don’t go according to plan, do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because you’re worried they’ll feel too unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things for that reason, no. I mean, I might choose the more familiar errand route because the parking is easier, but that’s not… fear, exactly. It’s just efficient.\n\nIn operations work you’re always trying to reduce unnecessary variability—though I suppose that sounds like control when I say it that way. But in daily life, if something might be unpredictable, I don’t think I’m staying away from it because of that. I can get annoyed if plans change, but avoiding whole situations? No, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that bother you so much that you feel you have to push them out of your mind or do something to neutralize them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can have a thought I’d rather not dwell on, the way anyone can—like remembering I forgot to return a library book years ago, which is silly, because I don’t even use that branch anymore—but I don’t feel like I have to do some special thing to cancel it out.\n\n“Neutralize” makes me think of chemistry class, actually. Or balancing an equation. But no, I don’t think I have thoughts or images that bother me in that particular way. If something nags at me, I might distract myself or get busy with something else, but not because I feel compelled to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing certain things over and over again—like checking, counting, or arranging—in a specific way that feels necessary to reduce discomfort or make things feel 'right'?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll check the door once before bed, but that seems normal—especially because my neighbor had a package taken once, and after that everyone on the block got a bit fussy about locks for a while.\n\nCounting… not really. I worked with numbers for years, so I do notice patterns, I suppose, like if tiles are uneven or if a spreadsheet column is misaligned, but I don’t feel like I have to fix it to make the world feel right. Usually if something unwanted comes up, I just shift to whatever I’m doing next. So I usually just say I’m tired or I have something to do at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images related to food, your shape, or control that upset you so much that you actively try to push them out of your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them mental images, no. I think about food and keeping things controlled, of course, but it’s more… planning, or checking in with myself, not some awful picture barging in that I have to shove away.\n\nSometimes if I’m in the grocery store too long I’ll get a bit irritated with all the displays—holiday cookies in September now, which I still think is ridiculous; when I was a girl they didn’t put things out months early like that. But I don’t feel like I’m fighting thoughts out of my head, exactly. I just move along to the next aisle or look at my list. So… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo your concerns about food and shape ever cause you such significant distress that you have trouble focusing on anything else, or do they ever make you feel so overwhelmed that you withdraw from activities or people?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / operations_research_analyst / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still keep up with my analysis work and errands, but I’ve mostly stopped joining friends for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, somewhat. I mean, I wouldn’t say I fall apart or anything—I still do my work, my spreadsheets and reports, and I get to the bank and the grocery store. I keep things moving.

But socially, yes… I’ve pulled back. After book club, everyone often wants to go have a meal, and I used to go. There was even one place where the chairs were terribly uncomfortable, but the soup was good—well, I suppose that’s beside the point. Lately I mostly don’t go, because eating out makes it hard to stick to the plan I rely on, and then I’m distracted the whole time anyway. So I usually make an excuse and go home.
